especially when I hear a phone ring late or see the glare of headlights on the highway, a sharp picture of the hospital corridor or the police officer’s voice just pushes into my mind and I have to pause to steady myself.\n- key: S071\n  name: Trauma_Related_Amnesia\n  description: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).\n  manifestation: What frightens me is that I can account for the funeral arrangements and the calls I made afterward, but parts of the police call and what exactly happened when I reached the hospital are simply blank, which is not like me at all.\n- key: S076\n  name: Inability_Positive_Emotions\n  description: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).\n  manifestation: During the prayer gatherings and even when my nieces tried to comfort me, I found behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.\n  manifestation: I have snapped at my leadership team over small delays and wording in emails, using a tone I would normally consider unacceptable, and then I feel embarrassed because they have not really done anything wrong.\n- key: S079\n  name: Hypervigilance\n  description: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.\n  manifestation: When I drive now, I keep checking the mirrors, the speed of trucks around me, and where I could pull off, family, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut during the prayer gatherings, and when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I noticed I was sort of… performing the right responses. It was almost like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in things you usually care about or enjoy, like hobbies, conversations, or time with family?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it losing interest, exactly… I still attend things, I still speak with family, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut during the prayer gatherings, and when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I noticed I was sort of… performing the right responses. I was polite, present, grateful. But inside, I didn’t feel the warmth or relief I expected to feel. It’s embarrassing to admit, but it was like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet for the moment. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or sad most of the day, nearly every day, since your cousin passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say it’s most of the day, nearly every day. I mean, I’m sad about him, of course, and it comes in waves… but I’m still able to get through the day and do what needs doing.\n\nI suppose I’m not sure where the line is, but “down all day” doesn’t quite feel accurate. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like hobbies, time with loved ones, or even small daily pleasures, feel less satisfying or meaningful since your cousin passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think maybe they do, a little. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still participating and I’m not shutting people out.\n\nBut at the prayer gatherings, and even when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I found myself doing what I should do—responding properly, being appreciative—but inside I didn’t really feel the warmth or relief I expected. It was almost like the affectionate part of me had gone quiet for a while.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as things feeling less meaningful, but that’s the closest way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what your mood is like when you’re alone, or during moments when there isn’t anything specific going on—like first thing in the morning or when you’re winding down at night?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone… I suppose it’s quieter, mostly. Not necessarily “sad” in a constant way. In the morning I usually just try to get myself organized and keep things moving, and at night I’m tired, so I don’t always stop to examine it.\n\nThere are moments where I feel a bit flat, maybe, but I don’t know if I’d call it a mood exactly. I’m still functioning. I hope that makes sense—I’m not sure if that’s the kind of answer you mean.\n\nDoctor: Looking back since your cousin passed away, would you say there have been days when you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, not just in response to reminders of the loss but more constantly, even when nothing else was going on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no. There may have been parts of days where I felt kind of flat or subdued, but I tend to connect it to something—thinking about him, or a reminder, or just the situation catching up with me.\n\nI don’t think I’ve noticed whole days where it’s just there constantly for no reason. At least, not in a way I’d feel confident calling that. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin passed away, have you had days where you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, even when there wasn’t anything specific reminding you of the loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not for most of the day, anyway. I mean, I’ve had moments where I’m subdued or kind of quiet inside, but usually I can point to something that brought it up, even if it’s small.\n\nI wouldn’t want to overstate it. I’m still getting through the day and doing what I need to do. So no, I don’t think I’ve had days where it’s just been there all day without a reason. A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s not that I can’t do things or be polite and present.

I’m normally very disciplined about sleep, routine, all of that.\n\nSince my cousin died, I find myself lying awake replaying that night, and then I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning with these scraps of hospital images in my head. I can still function, but I’m going into board meetings feeling like I’ve only slept in pieces, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more down or anxious than usual since your cousin passed away, or have these thoughts mostly just affected your sleep?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m… falling apart, exactly. I’m still showing up and doing what’s expected.\n\nBut yes, maybe more than just sleep. During the prayer gatherings, and even when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I was behaving properly, saying the right things, but inside I didn’t feel the warmth or relief I think I should have felt. It was almost like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in things you usually care about or enjoy, like hobbies, conversations, or time with family?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it losing interest, exactly… I still attend things, I still speak with family, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut during the prayer gatherings, and when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I noticed I was sort of… performing the right responses. I was polite, present, grateful. But inside, I didn’t feel the warmth or relief I expected to feel. It’s embarrassing to admit, but it was like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet for the moment. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or sad most of the day, nearly every day, since your cousin passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say it’s most of the day, nearly every day. I mean, I’m sad about him, of course, and it comes in waves… but I’m still able to get through the day and do what needs doing.\n\nI suppose I’m not sure where the line is, but “down all day” doesn’t quite feel accurate. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like hobbies, time with loved ones, or even small daily pleasures, feel less satisfying or meaningful since your cousin passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think maybe they do, a little. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still participating and I’m not shutting people out.\n\nBut at the prayer gatherings, and even when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I found myself doing what I should do—responding properly, being appreciative—but inside I didn’t really feel the warmth or relief I expected. It was almost like the affectionate part of me had gone quiet for a while.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as things feeling less meaningful, but that’s the closest way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what your mood is like when you’re alone, or during moments when there isn’t anything specific going on—like first thing in the morning or when you’re winding down at night?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone… I suppose it’s quieter, mostly. Not necessarily “sad” in a constant way. In the morning I usually just try to get myself organized and keep things moving, and at night I’m tired, so I don’t always stop to examine it.\n\nThere are moments where I feel a bit flat, maybe, but I don’t know if I’d call it a mood exactly. I’m still functioning. I hope that makes sense—I’m not sure if that’s the kind of answer you mean.\n\nDoctor: Looking back since your cousin passed away, would you say there have been days when you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, not just in response to reminders of the loss but more constantly, even when nothing else was going on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no. There may have been parts of days where I felt kind of flat or subdued, but I tend to connect it to something—thinking about him, or a reminder, or just the situation catching up with me.\n\nI don’t think I’ve noticed whole days where it’s just there constantly for no reason. At least, not in a way I’d feel confident calling that. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin passed away, have you had days where you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, even when there wasn’t anything specific reminding you of the loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not for most of the day, anyway. I mean, I’ve had moments where I’m subdued or kind of quiet inside, but usually I can point to something that brought it up, even if it’s small.\n\nI wouldn’t want to overstate it. I’m still getting through the day and doing what I need to do. So no, I don’t think I’ve had days where it’s just been there all day without a reason. Is that the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you usually enjoy, even things like a favorite TV show or time with a close friend, feel less enjoyable or like they don’t bring you the same satisfaction they used to?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. But inside, I didn’t really feel the warmth or relief I would normally expect. It was like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet for a bit.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s what you mean by losing interest or pleasure, but that’s the closest way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest or take less pleasure in most activities, even things you used to enjoy doing regularly, not just during specific moments like the prayer gatherings or when people are comforting you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say it’s most activities, no. I don’t want to make it sound broader than it is.\n\nIt’s more that in those situations where I expected to feel comforted or connected—like the prayer gatherings, or when my nieces were being so sweet to me—I could behave properly and I knew they meant well, but inside I just didn’t feel the warmth or relief. It was like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nSo… less pleasure in everything, not really. But in those moments of closeness, yes, something felt muted. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No, I don’t think I can honestly say it’s most of my usual activities, or nearly every day for two weeks. I wouldn’t want to answer that inaccurately.

What I have noticed is more specific… during the prayer gatherings, and when my nieces tried to comfort me, I could act normally and I appreciated them, but I didn’t feel the warmth or relief inside. It was almost like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.

Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin passed away, have you had days where you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, even when there wasn’t anything specific reminding you of the loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not for most of the day, anyway. I don’t think it was exactly most of the day, or completely unrelated to what happened… at least, I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly.

What I did notice was more in those moments when people were trying to be close to me. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still participating and I’m not shutting people out.\n\nBut at the prayer gatherings, and even when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I found myself doing what I should do—responding properly, being appreciative—but inside I didn’t really feel the warmth or relief I expected. It was almost like the affectionate part of me had gone quiet for a while.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as things feeling less meaningful, but that’s the closest way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what your mood is like when you’re alone, or during moments when there isn’t anything specific going on—like first thing in the morning or when you’re winding down at night?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone… I suppose it’s quieter, mostly. Not necessarily “sad” in a constant way. In the morning I usually just try to get myself organized and keep things moving, and at night I’m tired, so I don’t always stop to examine it.\n\nThere are moments where I feel a bit flat, maybe, but I don’t know if I’d call it a mood exactly. I’m still functioning. I hope that makes sense—I’m not sure if that’s the kind of answer you mean.\n\nDoctor: Looking back since your cousin passed away, would you say there have been days when you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, not just in response to reminders of the loss but more constantly, even when nothing else was going on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no. There may have been parts of days where I felt kind of flat or subdued, but I tend to connect it to something—thinking about him, or a reminder, or just the situation catching up with me.\n\nI don’t think I’ve noticed whole days where it’s just there constantly for no reason. At least, not in a way I’d feel confident calling that. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin passed away, have you had days where you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, even when there wasn’t anything specific reminding you of the loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not for most of the day, anyway. I mean, I’ve had moments where I’m subdued or kind of quiet inside, but usually I can point to something that brought it up, even if it’s small.\n\nI wouldn’t want to overstate it. I’m still getting through the day and doing what I need to do. So no, I don’t think I’ve had days where it’s just been there all day without a reason. Is that the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you usually enjoy, even things like a favorite TV show or time with a close friend, feel less enjoyable or like they don’t bring you the same satisfaction they used to?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s not that I can’t do things or be polite and present.\n\nAt the prayer gatherings, for example, I was there and I behaved appropriately, spoke to people, did what one does. And when my nieces tried to comfort me, I knew it was kind of them—I really did. But inside, I didn’t feel the warmth or relief I thought I should feel. It was almost like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet for a while.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as things feeling less enjoyable, but… it did feel like I wasn’t taking in comfort the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’re not getting any pleasure or interest out of things you usually enjoy, even small things, and that this has been happening most days for a while now?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say no pleasure in everything, exactly… I don’t want to overstate it. But yes, there have been times when I noticed something felt missing.\n\nAt the prayer gatherings, and even when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I was able to act properly and I appreciated that they were being kind. But inside, I didn’t really feel the warmth or relief I would normally expect. It was like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet for a bit.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s what you mean by losing interest or pleasure, but that’s the closest way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest or take less pleasure in most activities, even things you used to enjoy doing regularly, not just during specific moments like the prayer gatherings or when people are comforting you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say it’s most activities, no. I don’t want to make it sound broader than it is.\n\nIt’s more that in those situations where I expected to feel comforted or connected—like the prayer gatherings, or when my nieces were being so sweet to me—I could behave properly and I knew they meant well, but inside I just didn’t feel the warmth or relief. It was like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nSo… less pleasure in everything, not really. But in those moments of closeness, yes, something felt muted. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in most of your usual activities, even outside of grief-related moments, nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I can honestly say it’s most of my usual activities, or nearly every day for two weeks. I wouldn’t want to answer that inaccurately.\n\nWhat I have noticed is more specific… during the prayer gatherings, and when my nieces tried to comfort me, I could act normally and I appreciated them, but I didn’t feel the warmth or relief inside. It was almost like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nSo outside those kinds of moments, I’m not sure I’d call it a general loss of interest. Is that the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, even when you weren’t thinking about your cousin or any specific reminder of the loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was exactly most of the day, or completely unrelated to what happened… at least, I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly.\n\nWhat I did notice was more in those moments when people were trying to be close to me. At the prayer gatherings, and when my nieces were comforting me, I could behave the way I should, and I knew they were being loving. But inside I didn’t really feel the warmth or relief I expected to feel. It was like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to say I was just flat all day for no reason, if that makes sense. It was more that in those moments, something felt muted.\n\nDoctor: Outside of moments like the prayer gatherings or when people are trying to comfort you, have you noticed that things you used to enjoy—like hobbies, conversations, or time with friends—feel less pleasurable or meaningful than they used to?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed that, no—not in a clear way. I mean, I’m not doing everything exactly the same, but I wouldn’t say hobbies or conversations or being with friends feel broadly empty or meaningless.\n\nIt’s more like what I said before: in certain comforting moments, I expected to feel something and didn’t quite. But outside of that, I don’t think I can honestly say the usual things have all lost pleasure for me. Is that an okay way to answer?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really in the sense of a whole day, no. I don’t want to overstate it.

It’s more that, when I was at the prayer gatherings, or when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I could respond properly and I knew they meant well… but I didn’t feel that warmth or relief inside. It was like the affectionate part of me just went quiet for a while.

So I’m not sure I’d call it being down most of the day without a reminder. Does that answer it the right way?

Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like hobbies, time with loved ones, or even small daily pleasures, feel less satisfying or meaningful since your cousin passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think maybe they do, a little. It was almost like the affectionate part of me had gone quiet for a while.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as things feeling less meaningful, but that’s the closest way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what your mood is like when you’re alone, or during moments when there isn’t anything specific going on—like first thing in the morning or when you’re winding down at night?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone… I suppose it’s quieter, mostly. It was like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to say I was just flat all day for no reason, if that makes sense. It was more that in those moments, something felt muted.\n\nDoctor: Outside of moments like the prayer gatherings or when people are trying to comfort you, have you noticed that things you used to enjoy—like hobbies, conversations, or time with friends—feel less pleasurable or meaningful than they used to?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed that, no—not in a clear way. I mean, I’m not doing everything exactly the same, but I wouldn’t say hobbies or conversations or being with friends feel broadly empty or meaningless.\n\nIt’s more like what I said before: in certain comforting moments, I expected to feel something and didn’t quite. But outside of that, I don’t think I can honestly say the usual things have all lost pleasure for me. Is that an okay way to answer?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, even when you weren't thinking about your cousin or any specific reminder of the loss?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of a whole day, no. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more that, when I was at the prayer gatherings, or when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I could respond properly and I knew they meant well… but I didn’t feel that warmth or relief inside. It was like the affectionate part of me just went quiet for a while.\n\nSo I’m not sure I’d call it being down most of the day without a reminder. Does that answer it the right way?\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any times when your mood felt down or flat for no clear reason, not connected to thinking about your cousin or those prayer gatherings, even just for a few hours?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.10, F43.11, F43.13\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Thank you. I… I’m here mostly because my sleep has gotten a bit disrupted, which is unusual for me. I’m normally very disciplined about sleep, routine, all of that.\n\nSince my cousin died, I find myself lying awake replaying that night, and then I’ll wake up around 3 or 4 in the morning with these scraps of hospital images in my head. I can still function, but I’m going into board meetings feeling like I’ve only slept in pieces, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more down or anxious than usual since your cousin passed away, or have these thoughts mostly just affected your sleep?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m… falling apart, exactly. I’m still showing up and doing what’s expected.\n\nBut yes, maybe more than just sleep. During the prayer gatherings, and even when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I was behaving properly, saying the right things, but inside I didn’t feel the warmth or relief I think I should have felt. It was almost like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself losing interest in things you usually care about or enjoy, like hobbies, conversations, or time with family?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it losing interest, exactly… I still attend things, I still speak with family, I do what I’m supposed to do.\n\nBut during the prayer gatherings, and when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I noticed I was sort of… performing the right responses. I was polite, present, grateful. But inside, I didn’t feel the warmth or relief I expected to feel. It’s embarrassing to admit, but it was like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet for the moment. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or sad most of the day, nearly every day, since your cousin passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say it’s most of the day, nearly every day. I mean, I’m sad about him, of course, and it comes in waves… but I’m still able to get through the day and do what needs doing.\n\nI suppose I’m not sure where the line is, but “down all day” doesn’t quite feel accurate. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like hobbies, time with loved ones, or even small daily pleasures, feel less satisfying or meaningful since your cousin passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think maybe they do, a little. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still participating and I’m not shutting people out.\n\nBut at the prayer gatherings, and even when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I found myself doing what I should do—responding properly, being appreciative—but inside I didn’t really feel the warmth or relief I expected. It was almost like the affectionate part of me had gone quiet for a while.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as things feeling less meaningful, but that’s the closest way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what your mood is like when you’re alone, or during moments when there isn’t anything specific going on—like first thing in the morning or when you’re winding down at night?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone… I suppose it’s quieter, mostly. Not necessarily “sad” in a constant way. In the morning I usually just try to get myself organized and keep things moving, and at night I’m tired, so I don’t always stop to examine it.\n\nThere are moments where I feel a bit flat, maybe, but I don’t know if I’d call it a mood exactly. I’m still functioning. I hope that makes sense—I’m not sure if that’s the kind of answer you mean.\n\nDoctor: Looking back since your cousin passed away, would you say there have been days when you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, not just in response to reminders of the loss but more constantly, even when nothing else was going on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no. There may have been parts of days where I felt kind of flat or subdued, but I tend to connect it to something—thinking about him, or a reminder, or just the situation catching up with me.\n\nI don’t think I’ve noticed whole days where it’s just there constantly for no reason. At least, not in a way I’d feel confident calling that. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin passed away, have you had days where you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, even when there wasn’t anything specific reminding you of the loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not for most of the day, anyway. I mean, I’ve had moments where I’m subdued or kind of quiet inside, but usually I can point to something that brought it up, even if it’s small.\n\nI wouldn’t want to overstate it. I’m still getting through the day and doing what I need to do. So no, I don’t think I’ve had days where it’s just been there all day without a reason. Is that the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you usually enjoy, even things like a favorite TV show or time with a close friend, feel less enjoyable or like they don’t bring you the same satisfaction they used to?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s not that I can’t do things or be polite and present.\n\nAt the prayer gatherings, for example, I was there and I behaved appropriately, spoke to people, did what one does. And when my nieces tried to comfort me, I knew it was kind of them—I really did. But inside, I didn’t feel the warmth or relief I thought I should feel. It was almost like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet for a while.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as things feeling less enjoyable, but… it did feel like I wasn’t taking in comfort the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’re not getting any pleasure or interest out of things you usually enjoy, even small things, and that this has been happening most days for a while now?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say no pleasure in everything, exactly… I don’t want to overstate it. But yes, there have been times when I noticed something felt missing.\n\nAt the prayer gatherings, and even when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I was able to act properly and I appreciated that they were being kind. But inside, I didn’t really feel the warmth or relief I would normally expect. It was like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet for a bit.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s what you mean by losing interest or pleasure, but that’s the closest way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest or take less pleasure in most activities, even things you used to enjoy doing regularly, not just during specific moments like the prayer gatherings or when people are comforting you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say it’s most activities, no. I don’t want to make it sound broader than it is.\n\nIt’s more that in those situations where I expected to feel comforted or connected—like the prayer gatherings, or when my nieces were being so sweet to me—I could behave properly and I knew they meant well, but inside I just didn’t feel the warmth or relief. It was like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nSo… less pleasure in everything, not really. But in those moments of closeness, yes, something felt muted. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in most of your usual activities, even outside of grief-related moments, nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I can honestly say it’s most of my usual activities, or nearly every day for two weeks. I wouldn’t want to answer that inaccurately.\n\nWhat I have noticed is more specific… during the prayer gatherings, and when my nieces tried to comfort me, I could act normally and I appreciated them, but I didn’t feel the warmth or relief inside. It was almost like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nSo outside those kinds of moments, I’m not sure I’d call it a general loss of interest. Is that the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, even when you weren’t thinking about your cousin or any specific reminder of the loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was exactly most of the day, or completely unrelated to what happened… at least, I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly.\n\nWhat I did notice was more in those moments when people were trying to be close to me. I can still function, but I’m going into board meetings feeling like I’ve only slept in pieces, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling more down or anxious than usual since your cousin passed away, or have these thoughts mostly just affected your sleep?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’m… falling apart, exactly. In the morning I usually just try to get myself organized and keep things moving, and at night I’m tired, so I don’t always stop to examine it.\n\nThere are moments where I feel a bit flat, maybe, but I don’t know if I’d call it a mood exactly. I’m still functioning. I hope that makes sense—I’m not sure if that’s the kind of answer you mean.\n\nDoctor: Looking back since your cousin passed away, would you say there have been days when you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, not just in response to reminders of the loss but more constantly, even when nothing else was going on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin passed away, have you had days where you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, even when there wasn’t anything specific reminding you of the loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not for most of the day, anyway. It’s not that I can’t do things or be polite and present.\n\nAt the prayer gatherings, for example, I was there and I behaved appropriately, spoke to people, did what one does. And when my nieces tried to comfort me, I knew it was kind of them—I really did. But inside, I didn’t feel the warmth or relief I thought I should feel. It was almost like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet for a while.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as things feeling less enjoyable, but… it did feel like I wasn’t taking in comfort the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’re not getting any pleasure or interest out of things you usually enjoy, even small things, and that this has been happening most days for a while now?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say no pleasure in everything, exactly… I don’t want to overstate it. Is that the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, even when you weren’t thinking about your cousin or any specific reminder of the loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was exactly most of the day, or completely unrelated to what happened… at least, I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly.\n\nWhat I did notice was more in those moments when people were trying to be close to me. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you felt down or sad most of the day, nearly every day, since your cousin passed away?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say it’s most of the day, nearly every day. I mean, I’m sad about him, of course, and it comes in waves… but I’m still able to get through the day and do what needs doing.\n\nI suppose I’m not sure where the line is, but “down all day” doesn’t quite feel accurate. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that things you usually enjoy, like hobbies, time with loved ones, or even small daily pleasures, feel less satisfying or meaningful since your cousin passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think maybe they do, a little. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, because I’m still participating and I’m not shutting people out.\n\nBut at the prayer gatherings, and even when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I found myself doing what I should do—responding properly, being appreciative—but inside I didn’t really feel the warmth or relief I expected. It was almost like the affectionate part of me had gone quiet for a while.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as things feeling less meaningful, but that’s the closest way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Can you describe what your mood is like when you’re alone, or during moments when there isn’t anything specific going on—like first thing in the morning or when you’re winding down at night?\n\nPatient: When I’m alone… I suppose it’s quieter, mostly. Not necessarily “sad” in a constant way. In the morning I usually just try to get myself organized and keep things moving, and at night I’m tired, so I don’t always stop to examine it.\n\nThere are moments where I feel a bit flat, maybe, but I don’t know if I’d call it a mood exactly. I’m still functioning. I hope that makes sense—I’m not sure if that’s the kind of answer you mean.\n\nDoctor: Looking back since your cousin passed away, would you say there have been days when you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, not just in response to reminders of the loss but more constantly, even when nothing else was going on?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can say that, no. There may have been parts of days where I felt kind of flat or subdued, but I tend to connect it to something—thinking about him, or a reminder, or just the situation catching up with me.\n\nI don’t think I’ve noticed whole days where it’s just there constantly for no reason. At least, not in a way I’d feel confident calling that. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Since your cousin passed away, have you had days where you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, even when there wasn’t anything specific reminding you of the loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not for most of the day, anyway. I mean, I’ve had moments where I’m subdued or kind of quiet inside, but usually I can point to something that brought it up, even if it’s small.\n\nI wouldn’t want to overstate it. I’m still getting through the day and doing what I need to do. So no, I don’t think I’ve had days where it’s just been there all day without a reason. Is that the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that activities you usually enjoy, even things like a favorite TV show or time with a close friend, feel less enjoyable or like they don’t bring you the same satisfaction they used to?\n\nPatient: A little, yes… though I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s not that I can’t do things or be polite and present.\n\nAt the prayer gatherings, for example, I was there and I behaved appropriately, spoke to people, did what one does. And when my nieces tried to comfort me, I knew it was kind of them—I really did. But inside, I didn’t feel the warmth or relief I thought I should feel. It was almost like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet for a while.\n\nI’m not sure if that counts as things feeling less enjoyable, but… it did feel like I wasn’t taking in comfort the way I normally would.\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’re not getting any pleasure or interest out of things you usually enjoy, even small things, and that this has been happening most days for a while now?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say no pleasure in everything, exactly… I don’t want to overstate it. But yes, there have been times when I noticed something felt missing.\n\nAt the prayer gatherings, and even when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I was able to act properly and I appreciated that they were being kind. But inside, I didn’t really feel the warmth or relief I would normally expect. It was like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet for a bit.\n\nI’m not sure if that’s what you mean by losing interest or pleasure, but that’s the closest way I can describe it.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed that you’ve lost interest or take less pleasure in most activities, even things you used to enjoy doing regularly, not just during specific moments like the prayer gatherings or when people are comforting you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say it’s most activities, no. I don’t want to make it sound broader than it is.\n\nIt’s more that in those situations where I expected to feel comforted or connected—like the prayer gatherings, or when my nieces were being so sweet to me—I could behave properly and I knew they meant well, but inside I just didn’t feel the warmth or relief. It was like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nSo… less pleasure in everything, not really. But in those moments of closeness, yes, something felt muted. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in most of your usual activities, even outside of grief-related moments, nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I can honestly say it’s most of my usual activities, or nearly every day for two weeks. I wouldn’t want to answer that inaccurately.\n\nWhat I have noticed is more specific… during the prayer gatherings, and when my nieces tried to comfort me, I could act normally and I appreciated them, but I didn’t feel the warmth or relief inside. It was almost like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nSo outside those kinds of moments, I’m not sure I’d call it a general loss of interest. Is that the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, even when you weren’t thinking about your cousin or any specific reminder of the loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was exactly most of the day, or completely unrelated to what happened… at least, I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly.\n\nWhat I did notice was more in those moments when people were trying to be close to me. At the prayer gatherings, and when my nieces were comforting me, I could behave the way I should, and I knew they were being loving. But inside I didn’t really feel the warmth or relief I expected to feel. It was like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to say I was just flat all day for no reason, if that makes sense. It was more that in those moments, something felt muted.\n\nDoctor: Outside of moments like the prayer gatherings or when people are trying to comfort you, have you noticed that things you used to enjoy—like hobbies, conversations, or time with friends—feel less pleasurable or meaningful than they used to?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed that, no—not in a clear way. I mean, I’m not doing everything exactly the same, but I wouldn’t say hobbies or conversations or being with friends feel broadly empty or meaningless.\n\nIt’s more like what I said before: in certain comforting moments, I expected to feel something and didn’t quite. But outside of that, I don’t think I can honestly say the usual things have all lost pleasure for me. Is that an okay way to answer?\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days where you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, even when you weren't thinking about your cousin or any specific reminder of the loss?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of a whole day, no. I don’t want to overstate it.\n\nIt’s more that, when I was at the prayer gatherings, or when my nieces were trying to comfort me, I could respond properly and I knew they meant well… but I didn’t feel that warmth or relief inside. It was like the affectionate part of me just went quiet for a while.\n\nSo I’m not sure I’d call it being down most of the day without a reminder. Does that answer it the right way?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any times when your mood felt down or flat for no clear reason, not connected to thinking about your cousin or those prayer gatherings, even just for a few hours?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not really. I’ve tried to pay attention since you asked it that way, and I can’t point to a few hours where I just felt flat out of nowhere, separate from everything.\n\nI mean, I have quieter moments, but they usually still feel tied to what happened or to being around people who are trying to support me. I wouldn’t want to call it a mood problem if that’s not accurate. Is that the kind of distinction you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you found that you’re not getting any pleasure or interest out of almost all activities, even things you normally enjoy, and that this has been happening almost every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think that’s been happening. Not almost all activities, and not every day for two weeks.\n\nI’m a bit less engaged with some things, maybe, but I wouldn’t say I’ve lost pleasure or interest in everything I normally enjoy. That feels too strong. If anything, it comes and goes around certain moments, not like a constant state. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a loss of interest or pleasure in most of your usual activities, even outside of grief-related moments, nearly every day for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think I can honestly say it’s most of my usual activities, or nearly every day for two weeks. I wouldn’t want to answer that inaccurately.\n\nWhat I have noticed is more specific… during the prayer gatherings, and when my nieces tried to comfort me, I could act normally and I appreciated them, but I didn’t feel the warmth or relief inside. It was almost like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nSo outside those kinds of moments, I’m not sure I’d call it a general loss of interest. Is that the distinction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any days where you felt down or emotionally flat for most of the day, even when you weren’t thinking about your cousin or any specific reminder of the loss?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it was exactly most of the day, or completely unrelated to what happened… at least, I’m not sure I can separate it that neatly.\n\nWhat I did notice was more in those moments when people were trying to be close to me. At the prayer gatherings, and when my nieces were comforting me, I could behave the way I should, and I knew they were being loving. But inside I didn’t really feel the warmth or relief I expected to feel. It was like that affectionate part of me had gone quiet.\n\nSo I wouldn’t want to say I was just flat all day for no reason, if that makes sense. It was more that in those moments, something felt muted.\n\nDoctor: Outside of moments like the prayer gatherings or when people are trying to comfort you, have you noticed that things you used to enjoy—like hobbies, conversations, or time with friends—feel less pleasurable or meaningful than they used to?\n\nPatient: I haven’t really noticed that, no—not in a clear way.
